Convergence analysis of a family of Zermelo-type iterations for the Bradley–Terry model
Ruijian Han
Affiliation:
Department of Data Science and Artificial Intelligence, The Hong Kong Polytechnic University, Hung Hom, Kowloon, Hong Kong SAR
Ding Lu
Affiliation:
Department of Mathematics, University of Kentucky, Lexington, KY, 40506, USA
Yiming Xu
Affiliation:
Department of Mathematics, University of Kentucky, Lexington, KY, 40506, USA
August 10, 2026
Abstract
Zermelo’s algorithm is a classical method for computing the maximum likelihood estimator in the Bradley–Terry (BT) model, but its convergence can be slow in practice. To accelerate computation, Newman introduced a family of Zermelo-type fixed-point iterations parameterized by
α
\alpha
, with Zermelo’s algorithm recovered at
α
=
1
\alpha=1
. Empirical evidence suggests that the choice
α
=
0
\alpha=0
often converges substantially faster, making it a promising alternative, yet the mechanism underlying this acceleration remains elusive. This paper provides theoretical insight into this phenomenon through a systematic local convergence analysis. We derive closed-form expressions for local convergence factors under synchronous and asynchronous updates and analyze their dependence on
α
\alpha
via spectral analysis of the associated Jacobian matrices. For synchronous updates, we show that the algorithm may fail to converge when
α
<
1
\alpha<1
, and its local convergence factor is quasi-convex in
α
\alpha
under the population BT model. In contrast, asynchronous updates are always locally convergent, and their local convergence factor is provably monotonically increasing in
α
\alpha
under the population BT model of consistently ordered bipartite comparison graphs, establishing the optimality of
α
=
0
\alpha=0
in this setting. We further establish asymptotic approximation results for the population convergence factors under the BT model, justifying their practical relevance. Numerical experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets confirm the theory. Our analysis complements existing convergence results and shows that the acceleration of
α
=
0
\alpha=0
arises not only from the parameter choice but, more importantly, from the use of asynchronous updates.
1

The problem of ranking a collection of objects from paired comparison data is a fundamental task in machine learning and data science.
Given a set of
n

[

]

The Bradley–Terry (BT) model, introduced by

A classical method for computing the MLE (

\displaystyle\pi_{i}=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{ij}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}(w_{ji}+w_{ij})/({\pi}_{i}+{\pi}_{j})},\quad\text{for $i=1,\dots,n$.}

These fixed-point equations admit a unique positive solution (up to scaling) provided

Despite its guaranteed global convergence, the convergence of Zermelo’s algorithm can be slow in practice;

These algorithms are derived from an equivalent reformulation of (

(1.5)
When

, these equations reduce to (

A key observation in
[

]
is that, within this family of fixed-point iterations by (

α
=
0
\alpha=0
typically achieves the fastest convergence and significantly outperforms Zermelo’s algorithm in numerical experiments.
These results suggest that the
α
=
0
\alpha=0
variant may serve as a computationally more efficient alternative to Zermelo’s algorithm.
However, it remains elusive whether this choice is universally preferable and, more generally, what theoretical principles govern the role of
α
\alpha
in determining convergence.
The major goal of this work is to fill those gaps through a systematic local convergence analysis of fixed-point iterations
defined by (
1.5
), which we collectively refer to as
Newman’s
α
\alpha
-scheme
.
Specifically, we derive closed-form expressions for the local convergence factors
ρ
⁡
(
α
)
\rho(\alpha)
of these algorithms and investigate their dependence on the parameter
α
\alpha
.
One of our key findings is that the behavior of
ρ
⁡
(
α
)
\rho(\alpha)
depends critically on how the fixed-point iterations are implemented,
namely, whether the variables are updated
synchronously
(with all
π
i
\pi_{i}
updated simultaneously)
or
asynchronously
(with
π
i
\pi_{i}
updated sequentially).
In particular, we show that an asynchronous implementation is essential
for the
α
=
0
\alpha=0
variant to attain its superior performance observed in practice.
The choice
α
=
0
\alpha=0
alone is not sufficient: Under certain conditions, the corresponding synchronous iteration is provably non-convergent and therefore offers no acceleration over Zermelo’s algorithm.
Contributions.
Our main contributions are threefold.
1.
Synchronous iterations
: We establish the local convergence
factors
ρ
sync
(
α
)
\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{sync}}}(\alpha)
of the fixed-point iterations for (
1.5
)
in terms of the extreme eigenvalues of an associated Jacobian,
whose eigenvalues are all real.
For expected comparison outcomes from the BT model,
we show that the corresponding
ρ
sync
(
α
)
\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{sync}}}(\alpha)
is a quasi-convex function in
α
\alpha
, but is not necessarily
monotonically increasing.
With numerical examples, we demonstrate that
ρ
sync
(
0
)
\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{sync}}}(0)
can exceed
1
1
, indicating the
α
=
0
\alpha=0
variant
is non-convergent and provides no acceleration over Zermelo’s
algorithm.
2.
Asynchronous iterations
:
We show that the local convergence factor satisfies
ρ
async
(
α
)
<
1
\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{async}}}(\alpha)<1
for
all
α
>
0
\alpha>0
, implying the algorithms are always locally convergent.
For expected comparison outcomes from the BT model with a bipartite comparison graph in consistent order,
we prove that the corresponding
ρ
async
(
α
)
\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{async}}}(\alpha)
can be characterized by the
eigenvalues of an associated quadratic eigenvalue problem and is
monotonically increasing in
α
\alpha
, thereby establishing the
optimality of the choice
α
=
0
\alpha=0
in this specialized setting.
For general expected BT outcomes, we further provide quantitative bounds
on the convergence factors.
3.
Approximation by population outcomes of BT models:
We illustrate that the convergence factors of Newman’s
α
\alpha
-schemes can often
be well approximated using the population (expected) comparison outcomes
of an underlying BT model, thereby demonstrating the practical
relevance of our theoretical analysis.
We justify this approximation both numerically,
using synthetic data generated from BT models under various graph
topologies as well as real-world datasets from applications,
and theoretically, by
establishing rigorous asymptotic approximation results for the
convergence factors under the BT model.
Organization.
The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
introduces the preliminaries, including a detailed description of Newman’s
α
\alpha
-scheme under both synchronous and asynchronous updates and their associated matrix representations.
Section
3
and
Section
4
present our main results on the local convergence analysis of Newman’s
α
\alpha
-scheme under synchronous and asynchronous updates, respectively.
Section
5
establishes approximation results relating the
local convergence factors to their population counterparts under the BT model.
Numerical experiments are reported in
Section
6
, followed by
concluding remarks in
Section
7
.

Observe that each mapping

accounts for an individual asynchronous update from (

In practice, the asynchronous update (
2.7
) is empirically
preferred
over its synchronous counterpart (
2.4
), as it
incorporates newly updated components at each pass and may lead to
faster convergence
[
32
,
48
]
.
However, this empirical advantage is not yet fully supported by
theoretical analysis, and a systematic comparison between the two
implementations is largely absent from the literature.
Existing theoretical analyses primarily focus on the global convergence of the algorithms.
In
[
32
]
, it is proved that the
asynchronous iteration (
2.7
) is globally convergent, since
each component update
π
i
←
f
α
,
i
​
(
𝝅
)
\pi_{i}\leftarrow f_{\alpha,i}(\bm{\pi})
yields a
monotonic increase in the likelihood function
l
⁡
(
𝝅
)
l(\bm{\pi})
.
When
α
=
1
\alpha=1
, the scheme (
2.2
) reduces to Zermelo’s algorithm
[
50
]
, whose
global convergence is well established and can be interpreted from
different perspectives, including
the Minorization–Maximization (MM) framework
[
27
]
,
gradient descent optimization
[
46
]
, and its
connection to the Sinkhorn algorithm for matrix
balancing
[
34
]
.
However, these global convergence analyses do not readily
extend to the synchronous scheme (
2.4
) when
α
<
1
\alpha<1
,
and therefore do not fully explain the observed advantage of the asynchronous scheme.
In fact, as we show, the two implementations can exhibit fundamentally different
convergence behavior, particularly as
α
→
0
\alpha\to 0
in certain cases.
For example, the synchronous scheme may fail to converge when the observed outcomes deviate
substantially from the BT model assumptions (see
Section
6.1
for a
numerical illustration).
This contrasts with the common belief that the two implementations converge to the same solution
(see, e.g.,
[
48
]
),
which generally holds for
α
=
1
\alpha=1
.
To quantify the convergence rate and gain further insight into the effects of the parameter
α
\alpha
, as well as the synchronous vs. asynchronous update, a local convergence analysis is needed. In particular, based on the fixed-point equations in (
2.4
) and (
2.7
), one can express the local convergence rate using the Jacobian
𝑱
F
α
\bm{J}_{F_{\alpha}}
(which is computed in
Section
2.2
), and then a spectral analysis of this Jacobian would reveal detailed information on local convergence behavior. This is the focus of Sections
3
-
4
.

3
Convergence analysis of synchronous updates
In this section, we focus on the local convergence rate of Newman’s
α
\alpha
-scheme with synchronous updates. The study of synchronous updates is of interest in its own right and also provides the basis for understanding the local convergence of the asynchronous version discussed in
Section
4
. Our main finding is that the convergence rate is not necessarily monotonically increasing in
α
\alpha
, indicating that
α
=
0

may not always be the optimal choice. This contrasts with the observation in
[
32
]
, where
α
=
0
\alpha=0
is empirically observed to be optimal for the

version of the algorithm in a number of scenarios. These results highlight that synchronous vs. asynchronous updates can exhibit fundamentally different behavior with respect to the parameter
α
\alpha
, and the optimality of
α
=
0
\alpha=0
is intrinsic to the asynchronous update of the algorithm in certain scenarios.

4
Convergence analysis of asynchronous updates
In this section, we study Newman’s
α
\alpha
-scheme using asynchronous updates, where a single component
π
i
\pi_{i}
is updated at a time while all other components
π
j
\pi_{j}
with
j
≠
i
j\neq i
remain fixed. We assume a sequential update order for
i
=
1
,
…
,
n
i=1,\dots,n
, but the analysis extends directly to any fixed, but otherwise arbitrary, ordering of the component updates. As before, we focus on the local convergence analysis via fixed-point analysis. We begin with a closed-form expression of the local convergence factor and show that the asynchronous scheme is always locally convergent for
α
≥
0
\alpha\geq 0
, provided that
𝒢
⁡
(
[
n
]
,
𝒲
)
{\mathcal{G}}([n],\mathcal{W})
is strongly connected. This stands in contrast to the results in the synchronous setting from
Section
3
and is consistent with the global convergence result from
[
32
]
, which was obtained via a different optimization-based approach. Moreover, this explicit characterization also allows us to look more closely at the role of the parameter
α
\alpha
in convergence. In particular, for bipartite and consistently ordered comparison graphs, we show that the local convergence rate increases monotonically with
α
\alpha
, and for general graphs, it leads to a quantitative characterization of the possible range of convergence factors.

). In this context, a consistent ordering is a standard tool
— it requires updates to propagate consistently along a particular ordering, which ensures that the iteration matrix has real eigenvalues in certain cases and facilitates convergence proofs.

Remark 4.4
.

5

Several preceding convergence results are established for expected outcomes under the BT model. This idealization simplifies the theoretical analysis and enables a rigorous characterization of the local convergence behavior of Newman’s

The strength of these conclusions relies on the premise that the local convergence factors

6
Numerical experiments
In this section, we present numerical examples to verify our theoretical analysis and illustrate its practical implications.
We consider both synthetic test data generated from BT models with representative graph structures and real-world datasets from applications.
We will show that the convergence factor
ρ
¯
\overline{\rho}
for the expected outcomes often effectively captures the actual convergence factor
ρ
\rho
of Newman’s
α
\alpha
-scheme under the BT model. Moreover, the synchronous and asynchronous updates may exhibit
fundamentally different behavior as
α
\alpha
varies, and the relative advantage of asynchronous updates generally becomes more
significant as
α
→
0
\alpha\to 0
.
Unless otherwise specified, the
exact
MLE
𝝅
^
\widehat{\bm{\pi}}
used in the experiments is computed using Newman’s algorithm with asynchronous updates, terminated when the iterates
{
𝝅
(
k
)
}
\{\bm{\pi}^{(k)}\}
satisfy
‖
𝝅
(
k
)
−
𝝅
(
k
−
1
)
‖
2
/
‖
𝝅
(
k
)
‖
2
≤
10
−
15
\|\bm{\pi}^{(k)}-\bm{\pi}^{(k-1)}\|_{2}/\|\bm{\pi}^{(k)}\|_{2}\leq 10^{-15}
.
All experiments are carried out in Python on a MacBook Air (M4, 2025) with 10 CPU cores. The source code to replicate the simulation results is available at
https://github.com/Yiminithere/newman-algorithm-for-BT
.

Example 2
.
This example presents two representative cases in which the synchronous update in Newman’s algorithm (
α
=
0
\alpha=0
) fails to converge
and therefore does not provide any acceleration over the classical Zermelo’s algorithm (
α
=

\alpha=1
). In contrast, the asynchronous update remains convergent and may offer significant acceleration. These results further highlight the importance and effectiveness of asynchronous updates
in the implementation of Newman’s algorithm.
In the first experiment, we consider the bipartite comparison graph. In this case,
ρ
¯

(
0
)
=
1
\overline{\rho}_{{}_{\mathrm{sync}}}(0)=1
as noted in Remark
3.3
, indicating that Newman’s
α

-schemes with synchronous update may fail to converge when

=
0
\alpha=0
. In contrast, with asynchronous updates, the algorithm is guaranteed to converge for all
α

0
\alpha\geq 0
since

(

)
<
1
\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{async}}}(\alpha)<1
according to
Theorem
4.2
.
For comparison, we test asynchronous updates using both the consistent ordering (grouping objects within each part together) and a random ordering (using random permutation).
Our test problem is generated using the same setup as in Example
1
,
with parameters
p
=
0
p=0
,
q
=
0.5
q=0.5
,
L
=
10
L=10
, and
σ
=

\sigma=0.5
.
Figures

and

report
the local convergence factors of the algorithms
and the convergence histories for

=

,
1
\alpha=0,1
.
As expected,

(

)
=
1
\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{sync}}}(0)=1
(to 3 decimal places) and

(

)

\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{async}}}(\alpha)<1
.
Indeed, when

=

\alpha=0
, the synchronous update fails to converge,
while the asynchronous update remains convergent and offers
significant acceleration over Zermelo’s algorithm (

with speedup approximately

From the left panel, the convergence factor

. This monotonicity is lost under the random ordering, although the two curves remain quite close for larger values of

In the second experiment, we consider the circular comparison graph, where the objects are arranged in a cycle, and each object is compared only with its two neighbors (a specialized bipartite graph). We study a challenging case in which the comparison outcomes in
𝒲
\mathcal{W}
are
cyclic
, i.e.,
w
i
​
j
≠
0
w_{ij}\neq 0
if and only if
j
≡
i
+
1
(
mod
n
)
j\equiv i+1\pmod{n}
. The cyclic outcomes create a preference loop, with object
i
i
preferred over
i
+
1
i+1
,
making the ranking based on the strength vector
𝝅
\bm{\pi}
under the BT model less informative (as the BT model is stochastically transitive), and slowing down the convergence of algorithms. Indeed, it can be shown that such a cyclic
𝒲
\mathcal{W}
is unlikely to arise under the BT model (see
Appendix
B
for details), so the convergence analysis of Newman’s
α
\alpha
-schemes based on the expected outcomes of the BT model may not accurately reflect the actual behavior of the algorithms (in particular,
ρ
¯
sync
\overline{\rho}_{{}_{\mathrm{sync}}}
and
ρ
sync
\rho_{{}_{\mathrm{sync}}}
may differ significantly).
For the numerical test, we set the problem size
n
=
20
n=20
and sample the nonzero
w
i
​
j
w_{ij}
in the cyclic
𝒲
\mathcal{W}
as independent random integers uniformly distributed over
[
10
7
,
5
×
10
7
]
[10^{7},5\times 10^{7}]
.
This yields a strongly connected comparison graph
𝒢
⁡
(
[
n
]
,
𝒲
)
{\mathcal{G}}([n],\mathcal{W})
,
for which the MLE
𝝅

6.2
Real-world data
This section demonstrates the effectiveness of our convergence analysis on real-world datasets. Comparison outcomes

and a strength vector estimated from the MLE (

𝝅

. We show that the convergence factors

Another noteworthy observation from
Figure
5
is that the dependence
of the convergence factors on the parameter
α
\alpha
for the Vervet
monkeys, ATP, and ASSISTments datasets closely resembles that of the
homogeneous, clustered, and near-bipartite cases in
Figure
2
, respectively. Indeed, the Vervet dataset consists of a small,
well-connected comparison graph that is qualitatively homogeneous. The
ATP dataset, by contrast, exhibits clustered structures that may be
partially explained by generational separation among players. For instance, the gap between the
Agassi–Sampras rivalry
of the early 1990s and the rise of the
Big Three
(Djokovic, Federer, Nadal) in the 2000s results in two relatively well-connected sub-populations with sparser cross-era comparisons. The ASSISTments dataset is bipartite due to the item-response mechanism generating the pairwise comparisons.
7
Conclusion
This paper studied the local convergence of Newman’s
α
\alpha
-scheme
under both synchronous and asynchronous updates. We derived closed-form expressions for the local convergence factors and analyzed their dependence on the parameter
α
\alpha
through the population version of the BT model. For the synchronous update, we showed that the local convergence factor may exceed
1
1
when
α
<
1
\alpha<1
, leading to local divergence, and that it is a quasi-convex function in
α
\alpha
under the population BT model. In contrast, for the asynchronous update, we proved the local convergence factor is always strictly less than
1
1
, and that it is provably monotonically increasing in
α
\alpha
under the population BT model of bipartite comparison graphs in consistent ordering. These results complement existing convergence analyses of Zermelo and related algorithms for the BT model and, in particular, highlight the importance of the asynchronous update for implementing Newman’s algorithm with
α
=
0
\alpha=0
in order to achieve accelerated convergence over the classical Zermelo’s algorithm (with
α
=
1
\alpha=1
). We further established asymptotic results justifying the approximation of the local convergence factors by their population counterparts under the BT model and demonstrated the effectiveness of our theoretical
analysis through numerical experiments.
There are several interesting directions for future research. First, our analysis of the variation of the convergence factors is established for the population version of the BT model. To what extent these results extend to general comparison outcomes (e.g., under specific forms of model misspecification) remains an open question. Second, although extensive numerical experiments suggest that the convergence factor of asynchronous updates is often monotonic in
α
\alpha
, our current theory establishes this only for population BT models on consistently ordered bipartite graphs. Extending this analysis to more general comparison graphs remains a significant theoretical challenge. A related direction is to understand the impact of the update ordering in the asynchronous implementation for a fixed comparison graph and to design efficient update-ordering strategies. We hope that this work provides a foundation for addressing these questions in future research.

Xu was supported by start-up funding from the University of Kentucky and the AMS-Simons Travel Grant (No. 3048116562).
